Towards a geometry for syntax
Jonathan Sterling

Abstract
It often happens that free algebras for a given theory satisfy useful reasoning principles that are not preserved under homomorphisms of algebras, and hence need not hold in an arbitrary algebra. For instance, if M M is the free monoid on a set A A , then the scalar multiplication function A × M → M A\times M\rightarrow M is injective. Therefore, when reasoning in the formal theory of monoids under A A , it is possible to use this injectivity law to make sound deductions even about monoids under A A for which scalar multiplication is not injective — a principle known in algebra as the permanence of identity . Properties of this kind are of fundamental practical importance to the logicians and computer scientists who design and implement computerized proof assistants like Lean and Coq, as they enable the formal reductions of equational problems that make type checking tractable. As type theories have become increasingly more sophisticated, it has become more and more difficult to establish the useful properties of their free models that enable effective implementation. These obstructions have facilitated a fruitful return to foundational work in type theory, which has taken on a more geometrical flavor than ever before. Here we expose a modern way to prove a highly non-trivial injectivity law for free models of Martin-Löf type theory, paying special attention to the ways that contemporary methods in type theory have been influenced by three important ideas of the Grothendieck school: the relative point of view , the language of universes , and the recollement of generalized spaces.

1
Introduction

The purpose of this paper is to explain several ways in which the Grothendieck
school has influenced theoretical computer science, focusing on the
subdiscipline of
type theory
and the study of its free models.

1.1
Type theory and the
relative point of view

Type theory is, of course, the study of Types; but much like other important
scientific and philosophical categories such as Space and Number, there is not
a single definition of what a Type is. Although the field of type theory is
often said to have been born with Russell’s
investigations
(
Russell 1937
;
Russell 1908
)
into a syntactic way to avoid the
eponymous “paradox”, it must be said that type theory today has very little
in common with this early line of research. Type theory in the sense studied by
professionals is rather aimed to provide both informal and formal mathematical
language to speak of objects and structures varying “continuously” over a
base — in other words, to define the mathematical foundations to
operationalize Grothendieck’s
relative point of view
.

The
relative point of view
states that instead of studying (
e.g
.
)
schemes
X
in the absolute, we should always study
relative
schemes

X\in\mathbf{Sch}/B
for an arbitrary base
B
.

Category theory implements the relative point of view by means of

fibrations
; but this language is greatly obfuscated in
comparison to the simplicity of working with non-relative objects. The goal of
type theory is to reconcile the expressivity of the relative point of view with
the simplicity of the global point of view, by providing a language that makes
movement between different fibers (base change) seamless. Because type theory
is built up from very simple and abstract axiomatics, many categories of
interest possess
type theoretic internal languages
which provide
streamlined accounts of relative objects
(
Maietti 2005
)
.

Example 1.1

(Relative schemes, type theoretically)
.

We recall that a
relative scheme
over a scheme
B
is conventionally
defined to be a morphism
E
B
in the category of schemes. In
contrast, the type theoretic viewpoint turns the display of
E
over
B
on
its side: in the type theoretic internal language of the (gros) Zariski
topos
(
Blechschmidt 2017
)
, a “scheme”
B
is nothing more than a type
satisfying certain conditions and a relative scheme over
B
is nothing more
than a scheme
E\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
varying in a parameter
x:B
. The constraints of type
theoretic language
automatically
ensure that all the
E\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
can be
glued together to form a single scheme
\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{x:B}}{\textstyle\sum_{x:B}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{x:B}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{x:B}}{E\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}
and moreover
that the projection
\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{x:B}}{\textstyle\sum_{x:B}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{x:B}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{x:B}}{E\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}}
B
is in fact a genuine
morphism of schemes. In this way, type theoretic language more directly
captures the base intuitions of the relative point of view while minimizing
bureaucratic overhead.

1.2
Universes in type theory and category theory

Type theory replaces the display of relative objects
E
B
with

families
of objects
E\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
varying in a formal parameter
x:B
.
This is achieved by postulating an imaginary “object of all objects” or a

universal object
such that
B
-indexed families of objects can be
phrased in terms of functions
into
the universal object. To start with,
this idea of postulating an imaginary universal object seems quite dangerous; for
instance, if types are interpreted as sets then this postulate seems to imply a
“set of all sets” in which indexed-families of sets can be valued. More
concerningly, if types are interpreted as (
e.g
.
) topological spaces, to
postulate a universal object seems to imply a “topological space of all
topological spaces”, which makes even less sense than a set of all sets.

It is a fundamental result of the field of type theory, however, that the
extension of a given theory by a universal object in this sense is

conservative
.

Theorem 1.2

(
Lumsdaine and Warren 2015
;
Awodey 2018
)
.

\mathscr{C}
be a category with a class of morphisms

\mathcal{D}
stable under pullback. Then the Yoneda embedding

Example 1.3
.

The following are examples of categories
\mathscr{C}
equipped with a class of
maps
\mathcal{D}
satisfying the assumptions of
Theorem

1.2
:

1.

The category
\mathbf{Set}
,
\mathbf{Esp}
, or
\mathbf{Sch}
equipped with the
class of all maps.

2.

The category
\mathbf{Esp}
equipped with the class of fiber bundles, or of
trivial bundles,
etc
.

3.

The category of simplicial sets equipped with the class of Kan
fibrations.

. The presentation in terms
of precomposition is simpler to work with because it is
strictly
associative and unital
in relation to base changes.

It is the strictness of base change qua precomposition that allows us to
directly speak of the fibers of a parameterized object
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x:B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x:B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x:B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x:B$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mapsto E\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, since for any
f:C\to B
and
g:D\to C
the notation

E\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle f\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle gx$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle gx$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle gx$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle gx$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle f\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle gx$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle gx$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle gx$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle gx$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle f\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle gx$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle gx$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle gx$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle gx$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle f\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle gx$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle gx$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle gx$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle gx$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
cannot distinguish between “first composing
f
with
g

and then doing base change” and “doing base change along
f
and then base
change along
g
”. When base change is implemented by pullback, these two ways
to restrict
E
B
to
D
are distinct but linked by a canonical
isomorphism. The strength of type theory is to completely avoid the need to
manipulate such canonical isomorphisms without sacrificing rigor.

1.2.2
Grothendieck’s universes

As the terminology suggests, there is a great deal of similarity between the
idea of universal objects and Grothendieck’s universes, which he famously
employed in SGA 4 to deal rigorously with the size issues that can quickly
arise when using category theory to organize mathematics
(
Artin et al. 1972
)
. Indeed,
a given Grothendieck universe is a universal object for the class of maps in

\mathbf{Set}
whose fibers have cardinality strictly lower than a given strongly
inaccessible cardinal.

Grothendieck’s universes were defined in terms of set theory and the

\in
-relation, but subsequent developments by several authors (including
Bénabou, Martin-Löf, Hofmann, Streicher, and others) has led to a more
structural perspective on universe objects that is amenable to formalization in
an arbitrary category.
The most influential input has been that of Jean Bénabou who had introduced
already in his 1971 lectures the notion of a
universe in a
topos

(
Bénabou 1973
, §6)
, which is essentially a
full internal
subcategory
of the topos satisfying certain closure conditions, later
interpreted and developed substantially further by
Streicher 2005
and
several other authors.

Remark 1.4
.

Grothendieck seems to have been unaware that
Zermelo 1930
had already considered both his notion of universe and his
universe axiom
, as pointed out by
Hamkins 2022
; for this reason, it may be most fair to refer to “Zermelo–Grothendieck universes”. It seems most likely that Per Martin-Löf’s universes were conceived independently of Grothendieck’s and with different motivation — as one may deduce from the fact that the first version of Martin-Löf’s universe
(
Martin-Löf 1971
)
was plagued by the very antinomy that Grothendieck’s universes aimed to avoid. Subsequent developments in the categorical–algebraic understanding of Martin-Löf’s type theoretic universes were, however, deeply influenced by the work of Grothendieck and Bénabou.

Remark 1.6
.

A universe
\mathcal{S}
in a locally cartesian closed category determines an internal
category in
\mathscr{C}
, whose object of objects is
\mathsf{Tp}_{\mathcal{S}}
itself and whose
object of morphisms is the exponential

\pi_{1}^{*}\mathsf{El}_{\mathcal{S}}\Rightarrow\pi_{2}^{*}\mathsf{El}_{\mathcal{S}}
over
\mathsf{Tp}_{\mathcal{S}}\times\mathsf{Tp}_{\mathcal{S}}
.
The externalization of the internal category
\mathcal{S}

Definition 1.7

(Contextual class of objects)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a universe in a category
\mathscr{C}
with a terminal object; a class of
objects
\mathcal{X}\subseteq\mathscr{C}
is called
\mathcal{S}
-contextual
when it
satisfies the following closure conditions:

1.

the terminal object is contained in
\mathcal{X}
;

2.

if
C\in\mathscr{C}
is contained in
\mathcal{X}
and
p\colon\hbox to32.28pt{\vbox to7.16pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.75pt\lower-0.33063pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

Definition 1.8

(Contextual objects)
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be a universe in a category
\mathscr{C}
with a terminal object; an object
of
\mathscr{C}
is called
\mathcal{S}
-contextual
when it is contained in the
smallest
\mathcal{S}
-contextual class in the sense of
Definition

1.7
.

1.2.4
Grothendieck–Bénabou universes inside a topos

If
\mathscr{E}
is an elementary topos with a natural numbers object
N
, following

Bénabou 1973
;
Streicher 2005
we can define a notion of

Grothendieck–Bénabou universe
in
\mathscr{E}
that restricts to the
familiar notion of Grothendieck universe when
\mathscr{E}=\mathbf{Set}
.

Definition 1.9
.

A universe
\mathcal{S}
in
\mathscr{E}
is called a
Grothendieck–Bénabou
universe
when it satisfies the following conditions:

1.

dependent sums
and
dependent products
: if both

is the base change adjoint triple.

2.

: every monomorphism of
\mathscr{E}
is

\mathcal{S}
.

: for any

\mathcal{S}

in

, then

is classified by
\mathcal{S}
.

:

is

\mathcal{S}
.

:

is
classified by
\mathcal{S}
.

1.3
Abstract and concrete syntax of type theory

So far we have discussed type theory as a convenient notation for working with
relative objects in various categories. Most users of type theory will need
no more
than this informal perspective on type theory. In
order to more thoroughly justify these applications, however, type theorists
have rendered the interpretation of type theoretical notations in various
categories as part of a more general discourse on the
syntax and
semantics of type theory

(
Hofmann 1997
)
.

There are many ways to think about what a model of type theory ought to be, but
most of them take the form of categories
\mathscr{C}
equipped with additional
structure in
\mathbf{Pr}\,{\mathscr{C}}
, axiomatizing the scenario of

Theorem

1.2
. The syntax of type theory can be studied both
abstractly and concretely; the
concrete syntax
of type theory can be
defined in terms of a (very complex) formal grammar, but it is just as well to
define the
abstract syntax
of a type theory to be given by the
initial object in the category of models of that type theory. That abstract
syntax can in fact be constructed as a quotient of concrete syntax is a
consequence of the results of
Cartmell 1978
, later tackled in more
specificity by
Streicher 1991
and
Uemura 2021
. Renewed
interest during the past decade
(
Voevodsky 2016
)
has led to
several creative re-treadings of the ground first paved by

Cartmell 1978
.

1.3.1
Computerized proof assistants

One motivation to study the syntax of type theory is to facilitate its

implemention
in computerized proof assistants; these are tools into
which human beings can enter formal type theoretical expressions representing
mathematical objects and proofs and have their validity automatically checked.
In addition to assuring the validity of constructions and proofs, proof
assistants also assist with book-keeping tasks — such as displaying what it
remains to show at any given point in an incomplete proof.
Dependent type theoretic proof assistants such as Coq
(
Coq Development Team 2016
)
,
Lean
(
de Moura et al. 2015
)
, and Agda
(
Norell 2009
)
are now routinely used to
develop and verify the correctness of both old and new
mathematics
(
Gonthier 2008
;
Gonthier et al. 2013
;
Hou Favonia
;
Scholze 2022
)
,
and there are now very extensive and mature libraries of mathematical results
available
(
mathlib Community 2020
;
Mahboubi and Tassi 2020
;
Voevodsky et al.
;
Rijke et al.
;
Escardó and contributors
;
1Lab Development Team 2022
)
.

1.3.2
External
vs
.
internal equality

Type theory is a somewhat unique language, in that it contains two different
kinds of equality: external and internal. Type theory’s
external
equality
is simply the congruence under which assertions of the form
u:A
are
stable; in particular, when a type
A
is
externally equal
to a type

B
, written
A\equiv B
, we may assert
u:A
if and only if we may assert

u:B
. True to its name, external equality cannot be assumed or refuted inside
type theory; in other words, it is part of the
grammar
rather than the

vocabulary
of type theory.
2
2

2

External equality in our sense is
usually referred to as
judgmental equality
or
definitional
equality
; both the traditional terminologies carry some philosophical force
and subtlety that we do not necessarily intend, so we prefer our more neutral
terminology. We refer the reader to

Martin-Löf 1975a
;
Martin-Löf 1996
;
Martin-Löf 1987
for further discussion
of the philosophical aspects.
In a
model of type theory
(including the
initial model), external equality is interpreted as ordinary mathematical
equality between elements of the model.

The second kind of equality in type theory is
internal equality
,
which is part of the vocabulary of type theory. For every type
A
and elements

u,v:A
there is a third type
u=_{A}v
classifying
identifications
of

u
and
v
as elements of
A
. Internal equality is meant to correspond to
ordinary mathematical equality; so, for instance, if the notion of a

group
is formalized in type theory, the unit laws are stated in terms of
internal equality. Here we shall not dwell further on internal
equality, in spite of the fact that it has been the main topic of type
theoretic research for more than two
decades
(
Hofmann and Streicher 1998
;
Voevodsky 2006
;
Awodey and Warren 2009
;
Univalent Foundations Program 2013
)
.

1.3.3
Decidability of external equality

Although there are a variety of possible designs for computerized proof
assistants based on type theory, experience has verified that the most practical
approach is to ensure that the relation of external equality can be

automatically
checked by the computer without any intervention by the
user. This goal, however, places severe constraints on what kinds of equations
can be part of external equality — as it is easy for external equality to become

undecidable
if enough laws are
added
(
Castellan et al. 2017
)
. For this reason, type theorists
have accumulated a variety of design principles that tend to ensure effective
decidability — though it remains very difficult to establish decidability in
any specific case.

1.3.4
Running example: injectivity of type constructors

In addition to decidability, one of the key lemmas servicing the computerized
implementation of type theory is the
injectivity of type
constructors
, which is what allows an algorithm to universally decompose the
task of checking an equation like
A\Rightarrow B\equiv A^{\prime}\Rightarrow B^{\prime}
to
the task of checking both
A\equiv A^{\prime}
and
B\equiv B^{\prime}
: the injectivity
property states that the latter judgments are the
only
way that the two
function spaces could be equal. Note that injectivity in this sense does

not
imply that the
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\Rightarrow$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\Rightarrow$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}\mathclose{}{)}}
operator is a monomorphism in an
arbitrary model of type theory (indeed, doing so would rule out most semantic
models of interest!). Nonetheless, the injectivity property can be stated in
terms of
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\Rightarrow$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\Rightarrow$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow$}\mathclose{}{)}}
being a monomorphism in the
initial model

of type theory. In fact, we shall use this injectivity law as our running
example throughout the rest of this paper.

1.4
Normalization and injectivity, for free monoids

Type theorists have found that the most reliable way to establish both
decidability of external equality (
Section

1.3.3
) and injectivity of
type constructors (
Section

1.3.4
) is to devise a
concrete

characterization of equivalence classes of expressions in terms of

normal forms
, equipping the quotient of concrete syntax by external
equality with a more canonical section that is amenable to effective
computation. This process is referred to as
normalization
.

Normalization is better understood first in a simpler context; to that end, we
consider the theory of monoids below and a similar injectivity law that we
might wish to establish for
free
monoids.

1.4.1
The theory of monoids

The algebraic theory of monoids
subjects a nullary operation
\epsilon
and a binary operation
\mu
to
the following equational laws:

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\vdash\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\epsilon,x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\epsilon,x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\epsilon,x$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\epsilon,x$}\mathclose{}{)}}\equiv x

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\vdash\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x,\epsilon$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x,\epsilon$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x,\epsilon$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x,\epsilon$}\mathclose{}{)}}\equiv x

\displaystyle x,y,z

\displaystyle\vdash\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}},z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}},z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}},z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x,y$}\mathclose{}{)}},z$}\mathclose{}{)}}\equiv\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle x,\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle y,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle y,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle y,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle y,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle x,\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle y,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle y,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle y,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle y,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle x,\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle y,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle y,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle y,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle y,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle x,\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle y,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle y,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle y,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle y,z$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

1.4.2
Constructing the free monoid on a set

Given a set
A
, we may construct the
free monoid
on
A
by
taking a quotient of the well-formed expressions in the theory of monoids with

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\displaystyle A$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\textstyle A$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}{\mathopen{}{\lvert}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle A$}\mathclose{}{\rvert}}
-many additional constants. First we may inductively define the set

\mathsf{Expr}\,A
of expressions by the generators:

a\in A
\eta\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}\in\mathsf{Expr}\,A

\epsilon\in\mathsf{Expr}\,A

u\in\mathsf{Expr}\,A
v\in\mathsf{Expr}\,A
\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}\in\mathsf{Expr}\,A

Next we inductively define

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\sim$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\sim$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}\mathclose{}{)}}\subseteq\mathsf{Expr}\,A\times\mathsf{Expr}\,A
to be the smallest congruence for the operations above closed under the following rules:

u\in\mathsf{Expr}\,A
\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\epsilon,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\epsilon,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\epsilon,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\epsilon,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}\sim u

u\in\mathsf{Expr}\,A
\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle u,\epsilon$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle u,\epsilon$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle u,\epsilon$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle u,\epsilon$}\mathclose{}{)}}\sim u

u\in\mathsf{Expr}\,A
v\in\mathsf{Expr}\,A
w\in\mathsf{Expr}\,A
\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}},w$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}},w$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}},w$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle u,v$}\mathclose{}{)}},w$}\mathclose{}{)}}\sim\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle u,\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle u,\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle u,\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle u,\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle v,w$}\mathclose{}{)}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

Then the carrier set of the free monoid on
A
can be expressed as the quotient

\mathsf{F}{A}=\mathsf{Expr}\,A/{\sim}
. Because
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\sim$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\sim$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\sim$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\sim$}\mathclose{}{)}}
is a congruence, there
is an evident monoid structure on
\mathsf{F}{A}
and it is simple to show that
this monoid structure is universal in relation to monoids equipped whose
carriers lie underneath
A
.

1.4.3
Injectivity of scalar multiplication in the free monoid

There is a “scalar multiplication”
A\times\mathsf{F}{A}\to\mathsf{F}{A}

function on the free monoid sending
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,u$}\mathclose{}{)}}
to
\mu\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\eta\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}},u$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\eta\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}},u$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\eta\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}},u$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\eta\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}},u$}\mathclose{}{)}}
.
A monoid-theoretic analogue to our running example (
Section

1.3.4
)
would be to prove that the scalar multiplication function on free monoids is
injective. With our presentation of
\mathsf{F}{A}
as a quotient of
\mathsf{Expr}\,A
,
however, it is very hard to see that this is necessariy the case — as we do
not have any kind of a handle on equivalence classes.

The solution is to find an alternative presentation of the free monoid that can
be defined inductively without any quotienting; and such an alternative
presentation is referred to a
normal forms presentation
. In the case
of free monoids, there is a trivial candidate for the normal forms
presentation: the set of
lists

A^{\star}
of elements of
A
, which can
be defined inductively as follows:

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}\in A^{\star}

a\in A
m\in A^{\star}
a\lhd m\in A^{\star}

In other words,
A^{\star}
is the initial algebra for the polynomial endofunctor

F\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle X$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle X$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle X$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle X$}\mathclose{}{)}}=\mathbf{1}+A\times X
. There is no need to quotient
A^{\star}
;
the monoid operations are defined by the following equations, using the
induction principle of
A^{\star}
:

\displaystyle\eta_{A^{\star}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle=a\lhd\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}

\displaystyle\epsilon_{A^{\star}}

\displaystyle=\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}

\displaystyle\mu_{A^{\star}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}},n$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}},n$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}},n$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\displaystyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\textstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}}{\mathopen{}{[}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle$}\mathclose{}{]}},n$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle=n

\displaystyle\mu_{A^{\star}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a\lhd m,n$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a\lhd m,n$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a\lhd m,n$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a\lhd m,n$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle=a\lhd\mu_{A^{\star}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle m,n$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle m,n$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle m,n$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle m,n$}\mathclose{}{)}}

It is easy to show that
A^{\star}
satisfies the equational laws of the monoid
theory, again by induction on lists. But more importantly, it is possible to
deduce immediately that the scalar multiplication on
A^{\star}
is injective.

Theorem 1.10
.

The scalar multiplication function
A\times A^{\star}\to A^{\star}
sending each

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle a,m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle a,m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle a,m$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle a,m$}\mathclose{}{)}}
to

for structure map of

is the right coproduct inclusion, which is injective

Therefore to deduce from

that the scalar

\mathsf{F}{A}
is injective, it suffices to construct

between

and

; in fact,
it is even enough to exhibit
\mathsf{F}{A}
as a retract of
A^{\star}
, as depicted
below where the horizontal arrow is the unique homomorphism of monoids under

determined by the universal property of

:

\mathsf{F}{A}

\mathsf{F}{A}

we have

. From the

; because

is a retraction of

, it follows that
u=u^{\prime}
.
∎

2
Free models of type theory and normalization

The normalization problem for free monoids that we explored in

Section

1.4
is a particularly easy case. Unfortunately,
things become significantly more difficult when we move from simple algebraic
theories to full type theories, where we are trying to characterize the
equivalence classes of
types
by normal forms; the difficulty is roughly
that types and their normal forms do not (a priori) live in the same category,
in contrast to the situation with monoids where both elements and normal forms
are organized into sets.

2.1
Natural models of type theory

We have alluded in
Section

1.3
to the many notions of “model of
type theory”; here we will consider
natural
models

(
Awodey 2018
)
, a categorical reformulation of
Dybjer’s
categories with families

(
Dybjer 1996
)
.

2.1.1
Representable maps and natural models

The definition of a natural model involves the concept of

representable natural transformation
, which was incidentally
introduced by
Grothendieck and Dieudonné 1960
in EGA 1.

Definition 2.1

(Relative representability)
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a full subcategory of
\mathscr{E}
; a morphism
E
B
of

\mathscr{E}
is said to be
relatively representable by an object of

\mathscr{C}
when for any
\Gamma
B
such that
\Gamma
lies in
\mathscr{C}
,
the fiber product
E\times_{B}\Gamma
lies in
\mathscr{C}
, identifying
\mathscr{C}
with
the essential image of the Yoneda embedding

\mathscr{C}
.
3
3

3

Some previous
expositions required
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{M}}
to be small; nonetheless, the theory
develops much more smoothly if we only require essential smallness.

Observe that a natural model is really a special kind of
universe

(
Definition

1.5
) in a category of presheaves.

Exegesis 2.3
.

In a natural model
\mathbf{M}
, objects of
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{M}}
are referred to as

contexts
and morphisms are called
substitutions
. When

\Gamma\in\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{M}}
is a context, an element of
\mathsf{Tp}_{\mathbf{M}}\Gamma
is a

type

A\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\gamma$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\gamma$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\gamma$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\gamma$}\mathclose{}{)}}
that depends on a parameter
\gamma:\Gamma
; the
representability of
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{M}}
ensures for each type
A
over
\Gamma
,
there is a context
\Gamma.A
called the
context comprehension

classifying pairs
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\gamma,a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\gamma,a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\gamma,a$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\gamma,a$}\mathclose{}{)}}
where
\gamma:\Gamma
and
a
is an
element of
A\gamma
.

Definition 2.4

(Democratic natural models)
.

A natural model
\mathbf{M}
is called
democratic
when every object

\Gamma\in\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{M}}
represents a
\mathbf{M}
-contextual object in the sense of

Definition

1.8
.

2.1.2
Function spaces on a natural model

Further structures on a natural model can be imposed; for instance, function
spaces correspond to cartesian squares of the following form in
\mathbf{Pr}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{M}}}
:

\mathchoice{\textstyle\sum_{A,B:\mathsf{Tp}_{\mathbf{M}}}}{\textstyle\sum_{A,B:\mathsf{Tp}_{\mathbf{M}}}}{\scriptstyle\sum_{A,B:\mathsf{Tp}_{\mathbf{M}}}}{\scriptscriptstyle\sum_{A,B:\mathsf{Tp}_{\mathbf{M}}}}\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{M}}^{-1}A\Rightarrow\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{M}}^{-1}B
\mathsf{Tp}_{\mathbf{M}}\times\mathsf{Tp}_{\mathbf{M}}
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle A,B,f$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle A,B,f$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle A,B,f$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle A,B,f$}\mathclose{}{)}}\mapsto\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle A,B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle A,B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle A,B$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle A,B$}\mathclose{}{)}}
\mathsf{El}_{\mathbf{M}}
\mathsf{Tp}_{\mathbf{M}}
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\lambda_{\mathbf{M}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\lambda_{\mathbf{M}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\lambda_{\mathbf{M}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\lambda_{\mathbf{M}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathbf{M}}
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\Rightarrow_{\mathbf{M}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\Rightarrow_{\mathbf{M}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\Rightarrow_{\mathbf{M}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\Rightarrow_{\mathbf{M}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

It can be shown that a natural model can be equipped with function spaces if
and only if the corresponding universe is closed under pushforwards of product
projection maps (this is a restriction of the condition that a
Grothendieck–Bénabou universe be closed under dependent products).

2.1.3
The (2,1)-category of natural models

Natural models and their structured variants (
e.g
.
natural models with function
spaces,
etc
.
) all arrange into (2,1)-categories. Here we will not dwell on the
conditions for a morphism between natural models to extend to a morphism of
natural models with function spaces; the interested reader should consult

Uemura 2021
for more on this.

2.1.4
Free natural models: the abstract syntax of type theory

What is important for us is that the (2,1)-category of natural models (and its
structured variants) be compactly generated or
presentable
in the sense
of
Lurie 2009
and therefore have
free objects
,
i.e
.
the
bi-initial natural model with function spaces on some constants,
etc
. Note
that the bi-initial natural model of a given type theory is always democratic
in the sense of
Definition

2.4
.

2.1.5
From universes to natural
models

Let
\mathscr{E}
be a locally small locally cartesian closed category, and let
\mathcal{S}

be a universe in
\mathscr{E}
such that
\mathbf{1}_{\mathscr{E}}\in\mathcal{S}
. Furthermore let

\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathscr{E}
be a full subcategory closed under all contextual objects
with respect to
\mathcal{S}
in the sense of
Definition

1.8
. In this
section, we will define a natural model
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{S}$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{S}$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{S}$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}_{\mathscr{C}}
called the

externalization
of
\mathcal{S}
over
\mathscr{C}
. We define

\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{S}$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{S}$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{S}$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}_{\mathscr{C}}}
to be
\mathscr{C}
itself. The inclusion functor

Proof.

We must check that for any
\Gamma\in\mathscr{C}
, the fiber product of any cospan

\boldsymbol{h}{\Gamma}\xrightarrow{A}N_{\mathscr{C}}B\xleftarrow{N_{\mathscr{C}}p}N_{\mathscr{C}}E
is
representable. Identifying
\boldsymbol{h}{\Gamma}
with
N_{\mathscr{C}}I\Gamma
, we may assume
without loss of generality that
A=N_{\mathscr{C}}A^{\prime}
for some

\hbox to38.65pt{\vbox to7.85pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.15279pt\lower-0.33063pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.15279pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{}{}}{}{}{{}{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{{}}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.9 3.46 L 27.08 3.46}{fill:none} {{}{{}}{}{}{{}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{19.81941pt}{2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linecap} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-linejoin} \lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -3.04 3.57 C -2.49 1.43 -1.25 0.42 0 0 C -1.25 -0.42 -2.49 -1.43 -3.04 -3.57}{fill:none} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@closescope }}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {{

. The fiber product of

I\Gamma\xrightarrow{A^{\prime}}B\xleftarrow{p}E
is
contextual
with respect to

\mathcal{S}
by definition, and it lies in
\mathscr{C}
by our assumption that
\mathscr{C}

contains all contextual objects.
∎

We may therefore define the generic family
\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{S}$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{S}$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{S}$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}_{\mathscr{C}}}
of

\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathcal{S}$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathcal{S}$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathcal{S}$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}{\mathopen{}{\lceil}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathcal{S}$}\mathclose{}{\rceil}}_{\mathscr{C}}
to be
N_{\mathscr{C}}\boldsymbol{\pi}_{\mathcal{S}}
, which is representable by

Lemma

2.9
.

2.2
Injectivity of type constructors in free natural models

We now come to a precise version of our original discussion about
injectivity of type constructors from
Section

1.3.4
.

.

The answer to

is ultimately “Yes!”
(
Theorem

3.54
), but this is as difficult to prove as

Corollary

1.11
was easy. As we have alluded to at
the beginning of
Section

2
, the problem is that
although the collection of types is a presheaf on
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{I}}
, we cannot very
well define the collection of
normal forms of types
to be a presheaf on

\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{I}}
, as we will illustrate in
Section

2.3
.

2.3
Normal forms are not functorial in substitutions

The reason that a useful notion of normal form cannot be defined as a presheaf
on
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{I}}
is that normal forms must distinguish between

variables
and the things that can be substituted for them. For
instance, if
x:\mathsf{O}\Rightarrow\mathsf{O}
represents a variable, then

x\,\mathsf{yes}
should be represent normal form; but under the instantiation of

x
by
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\lambda z.z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\lambda z.z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\lambda z.z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\lambda z.z$}\mathclose{}{)}}
, the resulting expression
\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\lambda z.z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\lambda z.z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\lambda z.z$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\lambda z.z$}\mathclose{}{)}}\,\mathsf{yes}
should
not
represent a normal form — the normal form
representing for this expresion should simply be
\mathsf{yes}
.
It follows that the only way that normal forms could give rise to a presheaf on

\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{I}}
is if
variables
gave rise to a presheaf on
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{I}}
,
but we will see that this does not obtain.

Problem 1

(Variables do not form a presheaf on
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{I}}
)
.

If the collection of variables in the sense of
Definition

2.10
formed a
presheaf on
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{I}}
, then we could extend it to inductively define a
presheaf normal forms satisfying our desired laws. We might try to define

\mathsf{Var}_{\mathbf{I}}\in\mathbf{Pr}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{I}}}
to assign to each context

\Gamma\in\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{I}}
the subset of

\mathbf{Hom}_{\mathbf{Pr}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{I}}}}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\boldsymbol{h}{\Gamma},\mathsf{El}_{\mathbf{I}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\boldsymbol{h}{\Gamma},\mathsf{El}_{\mathbf{I}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}{\Gamma},\mathsf{El}_{\mathbf{I}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\boldsymbol{h}{\Gamma},\mathsf{El}_{\mathbf{I}}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
spanned by variables in the
sense of
Definition

2.10
. This definition, however, is evidently not
functorial in
\Gamma
: variables are closed under precomposition with
projections
\Gamma.A
\Gamma
and certain other maps derived from
these, whereas functoriality in
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{I}}
requires closure under
precomposition with arbitrary maps.

2.4
Models of variables and the method of computability

Although we have seen that the variables of a natural model
\mathbf{M}
will not
generally arrange themselves into a presheaf on
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{M}}
. Nonetheless, it is
possible to imagine them forming a presheaf on a
different
category —
perhaps, intuitively, a wide subcategory of
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{M}}
that has fewer
morphisms in it and thus induces a weaker functoriality condition.

If
\rho\colon\hbox to40.51pt{\vbox to8.33pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.68056pt\lower-1.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.68056pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{
{}{}{}}{{{{}{}{}}}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

represents the inclusion functor of such a
wide subcategory on which the collection of variables forms a presheaf, then
there is some hope for way to state define the collection of normal forms —
not as a presheaf on
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{M}}
but as a presheaf on
\mathscr{R}
. Of course, we
must be able to link normal forms of types to the actual types they represent,
so the collection of types
\mathsf{Tp}_{\mathbf{M}}
must be imported into
\mathbf{Pr}\,{\mathscr{R}}
.
This is easily done, however, by considering its restriction
\rho^{*}\mathsf{Tp}_{\mathbf{M}}\in\mathbf{Pr}\,{\mathscr{R}}
.

2.4.1
Models of variables over a natural model

The situation that we have intuitively described can be made more precise with
the following more general notion of
model of variables
.

Definition 2.11

(
Bocquet et al. 2021
;
Uemura 2022
)
.

A
model of variables
over a natural model
\mathbf{M}
is defined to be a
natural model
\mathbf{R}
equipped with a homomorphism of natural models

such that the induced map

\mathsf{Tp}_{\mathbf{R}}

is an isomorphism.

In the situation of
Definition

2.11
, then we may define
\mathscr{R}

to be the underlying category
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{R}}
. Models of variables over
\mathbf{M}

themselves arrange into a compactly generated (2,1)-category, and so we may consider
the
bi-initial model of variables
over
\mathbf{I}
. In this case,

\mathsf{El}_{\mathbf{R}}\in\mathbf{Pr}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{R}}}
plays the role of the desired presheaf of variables;
indeed, the bi-initiality property here corresponds to the
inductive

definition of variables (
Definition

2.10
).

Exegesis 2.12
.

The purpose of requiring
\mathsf{Tp}_{\mathbf{R}}
\rho^{*}\mathsf{Tp}_{\mathbf{M}}
to be an
isomorphism is to ensure that variables are classified by the same sorts of
types as terms, and that the underlying functor

Because we have a suitable notion of variable in

and then define

to
be a presheaf of normal forms of types and
\mathsf{El}_{\mathbf{N}}
to be the presheaf of
normal forms of elements. This proposal will fail almost immediately, however.

Problem 2
.

When
\mathbf{R}
is the bi-initial model of variables over
\mathbf{I}
, there are
simply not enough morphisms in
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{R}}
to build a model
\mathbf{N}
of the
full type theory (
e.g
.
with function spaces) over it. For instance, the
function space in
\mathbf{N}
between two global types would necessarily induce an
exponential between their context comprehensions in
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{R}}
, but this
structure is not present in the bi-initial model of variables.

A more promising idea to avoid
2
is to let
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{N}}
be
a suitable full subcategory of
\mathbf{Pr}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{R}}}
closed under not only context
comprehension from
\mathbf{R}
but also the image of

There is another problem besides the above with the idea of modeling types and
terms by their normal forms, no matter what ambient category we may choose.

4
below demonstrates that the problem of normalization for
type theory with function spaces is vastly more difficult than that of (
e.g
.
)
the theory of monoids.

Problem 4
.

In the presence of function spaces, the collections of normal forms cannot be
used
directly
as a model. Roughly, the problem is that we would need
to define (
e.g
.
) an application function that takes a normal form of
u:A\to B
and a normal form of
v:A
to a normal form of
uv:B
, but this is exactly
the problem we have been trying to solve in the first place — so we cannot
define this function until our proof is complete.

2.4.3
Tait’s method of computability

2
,
3
and
4
were first solved by Bill
Tait, simultaneously, when he introduced the eponymous
method of
computability

(
Tait 1967
)
, also variously known as
logical
relations
,
logical predicates
, or the
reducibility
method
.
5
5

5

In addition to Tait’s original contribution, several other
authors contributed greatly to the early development (and naming) of this
concept, including for example

Girard 1971
;
Martin-Löf 1975a
;
Martin-Löf 1975b
;
Martin-Löf 1971
;
Plotkin 1973
;
Plotkin 1980
;
Prawitz 1971
;
Statman 1985
.

Tait’s brilliant solution to
2
and
3
, phrased in
non-categorical language, was to devise a model in which a context is modeled
as a predicate of some kind on a syntactic context; and a substitution is
modeled by a syntactic substitution that preserves the corresponding
predicates. Because every construct in the model is tracked by something
syntactic, there is enough data to define a pseudo-retraction from the model
onto the syntax. By imposing a further condition that the interpretation of
every type be equipped with a
projection
onto normal forms, Tait solves

4
.

2.4.4
Freyd’s categorical reconstruction of Tait computability

In
Freyd 1978
, Peter Freyd rephrased Tait’s method into categorical
language as an instance of
Artin gluing
or
recollement
,
when he used it to give the first conceptual proof of the existence and
disjunction properties in the free elmentary topos
(
Freyd 1978
)
. Of
course, Artin gluing was first introduced in SGA 4 as a way to reconstruct a
topos from complementary open and closed subtopoi. Freyd considered only
gluings along the global sections functor, whereas Tait’s original situation
(and ours) requires a more subtle gluing involving the functor that arises from
a model of variables

. Scenarios of this kind were
first investigated categorically by

Jung and Tiuryn 1993
;
Altenkirch et al. 1995
;
Streicher 1998
;
Fiore and Simpson 1999
;
Fiore 2002
.
Our own “synthetic” approach to Tait’s method, to be detailed in

Section

3.1
, is obtained by combining the observations of the cited authors
with the viewpoint of the type theoretic internal language
(
Section

1.1
) of glued topoi.

3
Normalization by gluing for free natural models

3.1
Synthetic Tait computability for models of type theory

We have seen in
Section

2.4
that the normalization
problem for type theory hinges on the concept of a
variable
, and
introduced a technical notion of “model of variables” on a natural model
(
Definition

2.11
) that can serve as a matrix in which to define
the notion of normal forms. As we pointed out in
Section

2.4.2
, this is not enough to
prove that normal forms adequately represent the constructs of the bi-initial
natural model
\mathbf{I}
of type theory.

The fundamental issue, exposed in
2
and
3
, is that
any normalization model
\mathbf{N}
must be structured with a homomorphism

\mathbf{N}
\mathbf{I}
, which shall be seen to be a pseudo-retraction of the
induced universal map
\mathbf{I}
\mathbf{N}
by an application of the
latter’s universal property; concretely, this means that both contexts and
substitutions of the normalization model
\mathbf{N}
must not forget the contexts
and substitutions from the bi-initial model to which they pertain.

The reason the pseudo-retraction
\mathbf{N}
\mathbf{I}
is needed is the
same as in our simpler example for free monoids
(
Section

1.4
,
Corollary

1.11
):
the universal map
\mathbf{I}
\mathbf{N}
sends each piece of term
X
to a
construct of the normalization model from which we might expect to extract a
normal form, and the purpose of the pseudo-retraction is to ensure that the
resulting normal form is a normal form for
X
, rather than a normal form for
some other term.

Bill Tait’s solution to these problems was to define models that
compositionally instrument syntactic constructs with additional data, namely
the data of “normalizability” or “computability”. In this section, we will
see how the categorical reconstruction of Tait’s method of computability arises
naturally from the idea of formally gluing the constructs of bi-initial model

\mathbf{I}
along the restriction functor
\rho^{*}\colon\hbox to70.96pt{\vbox to8.36pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 12.67078pt\lower-1.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

onto data valued in the model of variables
\mathbf{R}
, leading to a notion of

3.1.1
The topos of computability spaces over a model of variables

Let
\rho\colon\hbox to36.46pt{\vbox to7.19pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.31247pt\lower-0.33063pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

be a model of variables over a natural model

\mathbf{M}
. We will first show each aspect of the model of variables translates
into the geometric language of topoi. In particular, the two categories of
presheaves
\mathbf{Pr}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{M}}}
and
\mathbf{Pr}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{R}}}
are topoi; in this paper, we are
careful to distinguish the geometrical and algebraic aspects of
topoi
(
Anel and Joyal 2021
;
Vickers 2007
;
Bunge and Funk 2006
)
, so we shall write

{\textbf{{E}}}_{\mathbf{M}}
and
{\textbf{{E}}}_{\mathbf{R}}
for the topoi corresponding the two
categories of presheaves respectively.

Notation 3.1
.

Given a topos
X
, we will write
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{{\textbf{{X}}}}
for the corresponding
category; for example, we have
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{{\textbf{{E}}}_{\mathbf{M}}}=\mathbf{Pr}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{M}}}
. We refer to an object of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{{\textbf{{X}}}}
as a
sheaf on

X
.

Observation 3.2

Our goal is to classify a notion of
computability space
that instruments
the constructs of
\mathbf{M}
with data from
\mathbf{R}
; the fundamental example of a
computability space would then be the space of normal forms of types: in this
example, one instruments types that live in
\mathbf{M}
with normal forms that live
in
\mathbf{R}
.
First we will define precisely what a computability space is, and then we will
observe that that we may construct a topos
G
by Artin gluing whose
sheaves are exactly the computability spaces.

(2)

Observation 3.6
.

Under the geometry–algebra duality, the co-comma topos
G
corresponds
to the comma category
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{{\textbf{{G}}}}\simeq\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle\mathbf{Pr}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{R}}}\downarrow\boldsymbol{\rho}^{*}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle\mathbf{Pr}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{R}}}\downarrow\boldsymbol{\rho}^{*}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mathbf{Pr}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{R}}}\downarrow\boldsymbol{\rho}^{*}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mathbf{Pr}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{R}}}\downarrow\boldsymbol{\rho}^{*}$}\mathclose{}{)}}
,
i.e
.
the
Artin gluing
of
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{*}
. Therefore, sheaves on

G
are the same thing as computability spaces
qua

Definition

3.4
, and morphisms between sheaves are exactly
morphisms of computability spaces.

Lemma 3.7
.

Both the open and closed immersions are essential,
i.e
.
we have additional
(necessarily fully faithful) left adjoints
\boldsymbol{j}_{!}\dashv\boldsymbol{j}^{*}
and

\boldsymbol{i}_{!}\dashv\boldsymbol{i}^{*}
.

Proof.

That the open immersion is essential follows from the fact that
\mathbf{Pr}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{R}}}

has an initial object; that the closed immersion is essential follows from
the fact that
\boldsymbol{\rho}\colon\hbox to45.98pt{\vbox to8.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.81807pt\lower-1.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{

is essential.
Finally, in an adjoint triple
F\dashv G\dashv H
, the leftmost adjoint is
fully faithful if and only if the rightmost one is.
∎

Lemma 3.8
.

In fact, we have a further right adjoint
\boldsymbol{j}_{*}\dashv\boldsymbol{j}^{!}
.

Proof.

Because
\boldsymbol{\rho}^{*}
has a right adjoint
\boldsymbol{\rho}_{*}
.
∎

Exegesis 3.9
.

Under the identification of sheaves on
G
with computability spaces, we
may examine the behavior of all the adjoints
\boldsymbol{j}_{!}\dashv\boldsymbol{j}^{*}\dashv\boldsymbol{j}_{*}\dashv\boldsymbol{j}^{!}
and
\boldsymbol{i}_{!}\dashv\boldsymbol{i}^{*}\dashv\boldsymbol{i}_{*}
. We first describe the inverse image functors:

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{j}^{*}\mathchoice{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\displaystyle E,\pi_{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\textstyle E,\pi_{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptstyle E,\pi_{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}{\mathopen{}{(}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle E,\pi_{E}$}\mathclose{}{)}}

\displaystyle=E

Exegesis 3.10
.

The additional left adjoint
\boldsymbol{i}_{!}\dashv\boldsymbol{i}^{*}
will play an important role; it
is uniquely determined by the property of sending representables
\Gamma
to a
space of “variable renamings” for
\Gamma
in
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{{\textbf{{G}}}}
. If we think of

\Gamma
as a context, then an element of
\boldsymbol{i}_{!}\boldsymbol{h}{\Gamma}
can be thought of
as representing a sequence of variables that can be substituted for those
classified by
\Gamma
.

Lemma 3.11
.

The following square commutes up to isomorphism:

\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{R}}
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{M}}
\mathbf{Pr}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{R}}}
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{{\textbf{{G}}}}
\mathbf{Pr}\,{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{M}}}
\boldsymbol{h}_{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{R}}}
\boldsymbol{i}_{!}
\rho
\boldsymbol{h}_{\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}}_{\mathbf{M}}}
\boldsymbol{j}^{*}

3.1.2
Recollement of computability spaces

SGA 4 explains how the construction of
G
by gluing along

\boldsymbol{\rho}\colon\hbox to45.98pt{\vbox to8.44pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.81807pt\lower-1.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

corresponds, in reverse, to the

partitioning
of the topos
G
into complementary open and closed
subtopos
(
Artin et al. 1972
)
. Under the latter viewpoint, the open and closed
immersions become identified with the
inclusion
of the corresponding
open and closed subtopoi. We will use this perspective to develop a more
convenient language for constructing computability spaces intrinsically in the
language of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{{\textbf{{G}}}}
without bothering with the complex families of
presheaves by which we originally defined computability spaces
(
Definition

3.4
).

Definition 3.12

(Opens of a topos)
.

An
open
of a topos
X
is defined to be a subterminal sheaf on
that topos,
i.e
.
a subobject of
\mathbf{1}_{\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{{\textbf{{X}}}}}
. We will write

\boldsymbol{\mathcal{O}}_{{\textbf{{X}}}}\subseteq\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{{\textbf{{X}}}}
for the poset (frame, in fact) of opens of

X
.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
U\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{O}}_{{\textbf{{X}}}}
be an open of a topos
X
; then a sheaf
E
is
called
U
-modal
when the canonical map
E
E^{U}
is an
isomorphism. Conversely, a sheaf
E
is called
U
-connected
when
the projection map
E\times U
U
is an isomorphism.

Fact 3.14

(
U
-modal and
U
-connected reflection)
.

For an open
U\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{O}}_{{\textbf{{X}}}}
, the full subcategories of
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{{\textbf{{X}}}}

spanned by
U
-modal and
U
-connected sheaves are reflective.

1.

The
U
-modal reflection of a sheaf
X\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{{\textbf{{X}}}}
is given by the exponential
X^{U}
.

2.

The
U
-connected reflection of a sheaf
X\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{{\textbf{{X}}}}
is given by the pushout of the product span
X\xleftarrow{\pi_{1}}X\times U\xrightarrow{\pi_{2}}U
.

The
U
-modal and
U
-connected reflections preserve finite limits.
Therefore, the full subcategories of
U
-modal and
U
-connected sheaves
present
subtopoi
; the subtopos of
U
-modal sheaves is referred to as
the
open subtopos
determined by
U
and the subtopos of

U
-connected sheaves is referred to as the
closed subtopos

determined by
U
.

Fact 3.15

(Open subtopos as slice)
.

Based on the definition of the
U
-modal reflector, it is not difficult to
see that the slice category
\boldsymbol{\mathcal{S}}_{{\textbf{{X}}}}/U
may be canonically
identified with the category of sheaves on the open subtopos of
X

determined by
U
.

Observation 3.16

(Lawvere–Tierney topologies)
.

The open and closed subtopi can equivalently be described by Lawvere–Tierney
topologies on
X
, which simply internalize the reflectors as endomaps of
the subobject classifier.

1.

The topology of the open subtopos is given by the map
j_{/U}\phi=U\Rightarrow\phi
.

2.

The topology of the closed subtopos is given by
j_{\setminus{U}}\phi=U\lor\phi

Construction 3.17

(Recollement of the topos of computability spaces)
.

What we take from SGA 4
(
Artin et al. 1972
)
is that up to categorical equivalence,
we may reconstruct the gluing data for our own topos
G
from a certain
open
\Phi\in\boldsymbol{\mathcal{O}}_{{\textbf{{G}}}}
, which can be equivalently described by either
\Phi=\boldsymbol{i}_{*}\bot
or
\Phi=\boldsymbol{j}_{!}\top
. As a subterminal computability space,
\Phi
is
the family
